- Only about 1 in 18 weekly ChatGPT users paid as of Feb 2026 (50M of 900M). Across nine countries, only 18% of people have any paid AI access, so most people judge AI from free models.
- Paid users were about 3.5x as likely as free-only users to have used an AI agent for a real task in the past week (39% vs 11%).
- 91% of Anthropic staff, but only 39% of US adults, expect AI capable of Nobel-level discovery by 2030. 19-40% of the public say AI will never be able to do a given professional task.
- Young Americans have flipped. 55% of 18-29s are now more concerned than excited about AI (Pew 2026), up from 31% in 2021, and 47% say AI does more harm than good (Gallup 2026).
- AI experts in the 2023 AI Impacts survey put HLMI at 2047 but full automation of labor at 2116, a 69-year gap that comes purely from how the question is worded.
- Forecasters underestimated frontier-lab revenue by about 2x in 2025 (forecast $16B vs actual $30.4B), and end-2026 ARR forecasts ($20-25B) are about 4-5x below current reported values.
- About two-thirds of Americans use AI-enabled products without realizing they are AI (Gallup, Jan 2025).

### counterevidence

- METR's 2025 RCT: experienced developers were 19% slower with AI (95% CI +2% to +39%) while believing they were 20% faster. Self-assessed AI value can be badly wrong in the optimistic direction.
- METR's 2026 follow-up is inconclusive (CIs cross zero), and METR explicitly calls it an unreliable signal. There is still no clean measured speedup from METR.
- The Danish administrative data (Humlum & Vestergaard 2025) rule out earnings or hours effects larger than 2%, with about 3% average time savings.
- Remote Labor Index: even the best agent today is judged at least as good as a human on only about 21% of real freelance projects (2.5% at launch in Oct 2025).
- Forecasters overestimated SWE-Bench Verified for 2025 (88% forecast vs 80.9% actual), LLM bio-uplift (16-40% forecast vs 5.2% actual), and US robotaxi share for 2027 (expert forecast 7.3% vs trend about 2.5%).
- The LEAP METR time-horizon forecast for end-2026 (3.4 h at 80% success) is not yet beaten as of May 2026 (about 3.1 h), so not every forecast is being blown past.
- Hinton's 2016 prediction that radiologists would be obsolete within 5 years failed; radiologist demand and pay rose (Mayo radiology staff reportedly up about 55%; average pay about $571k per Fortune).
- FRI notes its own accuracy review is structurally biased toward detecting underestimation.
- Measured public skepticism isn't only ignorance. Gallup and SCSP find that more knowledge and use of AI are not associated with more positive sentiment.
- Reuters Institute: only 21% of users say they use AI because its information is more reliable than other sources. Pew: about half of those who get news from chatbots see inaccurate news.
- Some FrontierMath results are muddied by benchmark errors (Epoch found errors in about 42% of problems, per FRI), which complicates 'faster than predicted' claims.
- AI-washing enforcement cases show real overclaiming in the market, including a startup (Nate) allegedly using humans behind claimed AI.

### chart_ideas

- Forecast vs actual dumbbell chart: LEAP/XPT/AI Digest forecasts vs outcomes for FrontierMath (31% vs 40.7%), LiveCodeBench Pro Hard (14% vs 53.8%), Cybench (62% vs 82%), SWE-Bench Verified (88% vs 80.9%, an overestimate), bio-uplift (22.5% vs 5.2%, an overestimate), lab revenue ($16B vs $30.4B). Data: FRI accuracy update and AI Digest 2025 results.
- Timeline: years predicted vs actual for IMO gold (experts 2030, superforecasters 2035, actual 2025) and VCT virologist-level (experts 2030, superforecasters 2034, actual 2025). Data: FRI.
- Pew 'more concerned than excited' line, 2021-2026, overall and for ages 18-29 (37/38/52/51/50/52 overall; 31/29/42/39/47/55 for 18-29). Data: Pew Aug 18, 2026 short read.
- METR perception vs reality bar chart: forecast +24% speed, perceived +20%, measured -19% (slower). Add the 2026 follow-up estimates with wide CIs. Data: METR July 2025 and Feb 2026 posts.
- Paid vs free stacked bar: 18% paid access vs about 82% none (Reuters 2026), with agent use 39% vs 11% for paid vs free-only users. Data: Reuters Institute 2026.
- Insider vs public expectation bars: Nobel-level discovery by 2030 (91% vs 39%); share of own work AI can do by 2030 (75% vs 28%). Data: Anthropic AI expectations gap (label clearly as vendor research).
- Expert vs public optimism (Pew 2024 data): jobs 73 vs 23, economy 69 vs 21, medical care 84 vs 44, US overall 56 vs 17. Data: Pew Apr 2025 / AI Index 2026 ch.9.
- Remote Labor Index automation rate over time: 2.5% (Oct 2025) to about 21% (Nov 2026). Data: Scale RLI leaderboard (no CIs).
- Reuters six-country weekly generative-AI use, 2024-2026 (18%, 34%, 51%) and daily use (7%, 16%, 28%). Data: Reuters Institute 2025/2026 reports.

### open_questions

- No 2025 or 2026 edition of the AI Impacts researcher survey was found; latest is the 2023 survey (published Jan 2024).
- No primary data directly comparing capability perceptions of free vs paid users. The Reuters 2026 report breaks out agent use by paid status but not, as far as read, perception or limitation scores.
- METR's 2026 developer self-report survey (reported median 3x speedup, 349 workers) was only seen in secondary blogs; not verified on METR's site.
- The exact ChatGPT WAU after Feb 2026 is uncertain (1B in Aug 2026; 1.2B at DevDay possibly all OpenAI products). No updated paid-subscriber count found.
- The Bentley-Gallup article did not list per-task 2023 vs 2026 percentages; the full Gallup report would be needed.
- The Metaculus AGI community forecast's current value conflicts across secondary trackers (Jan 2033 vs Sep 2030); check Metaculus question 5121 directly.
- The Pew Apr 2025 'jobs 73% vs 23%' expert-public figure is confirmed via the AI Index 2026 chapter page but was not checked on Pew's own page.
- The MIT NANDA '95% of genAI pilots see no return' claim (Aug 2025) has disputed methodology and inconsistent sample sizes; the primary report wasn't accessed. Treat it with caution or omit.
- FTC AI-washing case counts since 2025 conflict between sources.
- The OpenAI Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize claim is described by FRI as not fully confirmed.
- No 2025-2026 Ipsos AI Monitor primary page was fetched; the global benefits/nervous figures come from the AI Index chapter, which doesn't name its source on that page.

## agi_metric

The evidence supports the author's belief (3), that "AGI = human-level" is a weak yardstick, but the reason is more specific than "it doesn't matter." The term has no stable definition. The major definitions measure different things: OpenAI's charter uses economic output, DeepMind's Levels of AGI uses percentiles of skilled humans, Hendrycks et al. use psychometric cognitive breadth, and ARC uses learning efficiency. Legally, the word has also lost its meaning. The October 2025 Microsoft–OpenAI deal made an independent expert panel responsible for verifying any AGI declaration. The April 27, 2026 amendment then made OpenAI's revenue-share payments "independent of OpenAI's technology progress" through 2030, which Simon Willison and The Verge read as killing the AGI clause. Insiders' statements are inconsistent too. Altman called AGI "not a super useful term" in August 2025, then told TIME in August 2026 that OpenAI would have an internal system he would call AGI by year-end. Brockman said in September 2026 at the GPT-6 Astra launch that it is "not unreasonable" to feel the AGI era has begun. Karpathy (October 2025) said AGI is "a decade away," and Amodei avoids the term as carrying "sci-fi baggage and hype."

The 'jagged' pattern runs through all the data. Hendrycks et al. score GPT-5 at 57%: 10/10 in reading-writing and math, but 0 in long-term memory storage. GPT-6 Astra beat the human action-efficiency baseline on 96% of ARC-AGI-3 levels under a special harness, yet scores 62.7% under ARC's standard harness. Astra also does worse than its predecessor on Artificial Analysis's GDPval-AA work tasks. The better yardsticks are continuous and task-anchored. On METR time horizons, the top 50% horizon is an early Claude Mythos Preview at about 17.4 hours, with a 95% CI of 8.5–55 hours, and METR warns that anything above 16 hours is unreliable on its current suite. GDPval wins-or-ties versus experts went from 47.6% for Claude Opus 4.1 in September 2025 to about 85% for GPT-5.5 per OpenAI (vendor-reported). The Remote Labor Index top automation rate went from 2.5% to 21.25% for Opus 5.5. Epoch's ECI is about 166 for Astra as of September 2026. Economic measures such as Anthropic's observed exposure show a large gap between capability and use: in Computer & Math, about 94% of tasks are theoretically feasible but only about 33% are actually covered. They also show no detectable rise in unemployment so far.

For beliefs (1) and (2), the counterevidence is that the strongest "real work" measures still show AI fully automating only about one in five paid freelance projects. Several benchmarks are saturating, so they can no longer measure the frontier. Many headline numbers are vendor-reported or depend on the harness used. One caveat for the piece: nearly every alternative yardstick (GDPval, RLI, METR, ARC) still uses human performance as its reference point. The better framing is "human-relative, task by task," not "no humans in the yardstick."

### Fact-check verdict

The findings hold up well against primary sources. I fetched and confirmed these exactly: the Microsoft blog posts from October 2025 and April 2026, Willison's post, the Levels of AGI table and version history, Hendrycks Table 1, the ARC Prize Astra post and leaderboard, the TIME article, the CNBC article, the Karpathy transcript, the Amodei essay, METR's v1.1 YAML and page, and the METR limitations note. I found nothing that looks fabricated. Corrections needed: (1) the BCG 7% is of individual-contributor-level consultants, and I could not verify the about 84% baseline or the Organization Science DOI; (2) the 96% ARC action-efficiency figure is the Provider Adapter at MAX effort, while 99.9% is at HIGH; (3) METR's doubling-time fit excludes the Mythos point (p50 above 16h); (4) Altman's CNBC remark aired before the Aug 11, 2025 article date. Still unverified, and to be held back or attributed carefully: the Brockman 'AGI era' quote (primary source not reachable), the CNBC 20% rate, the Feb 2026 joint statement, the ARC-AGI-3 launch-week scores and Chollet's figures. The bigger gap is the economic figures in the summary (GDPval 85%, RLI 21.25%, ECI 166, GDPval-AA, Anthropic exposure 94%/33%), which were never submitted for checking. Several are vendor-reported and must be sourced or labeled before publishing. The leaderboard also shows newer models (GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 4 Argon) that the briefing ignores.

- **confirmed**: OpenAI's charter defines AGI by economic output → "highly autonomous systems that outperform humans at most economically valuable work"; Charter dated April 9, 2018 (https://openai.com/charter/). openai.com returned 403 to my fetch. The wording and date are the long-standing, widely reproduced charter text, and Willison's history post also traces OpenAI's AGI language. The definition is not quantified anywhere. Low risk.
- **confirmed**: The October 2025 Microsoft–OpenAI deal moved AGI verification to an independent expert panel → The declaration "will now be verified by an independent expert panel". Model/product IP runs through 2032 and includes post-AGI models, with safety guardrails. Research IP lasts until the panel verifies AGI "or through 2030, whichever is first". The revenue share continues "until the expert panel verifies AGI", with payments spread over a longer period. (https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2025/10/28/the-next-chapter-of-the-microsoft-openai-partnership/). Fetched the primary post and the wording matches. The framing that 'the board could make the call alone' before this is interpretation, not quoted from the post; attribute it or soften it. The post adds that payments are spread over a longer period, which the researcher omitted.
- **confirmed**: The April 27, 2026 amendment made OpenAI's payments to Microsoft independent of AGI; commentators read this as the end of the AGI clause → License to OpenAI IP for models and products through 2032, now non-exclusive. Microsoft no longer pays OpenAI a revenue share. OpenAI's payments continue through 2030 "independent of OpenAI's technology progress", at the same percentage, subject to a total cap (amount not disclosed). The post does not mention AGI or the panel. (https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2026/04/27/the-next-phase-of-the-microsoft-openai-partnership/). Primary post fetched. Willison's post 'Tracking the history of the now-deceased OpenAI Microsoft AGI clause' (Apr 27, 2026) confirms the 'dead' reading and cites The Verge. Not verified: the CNBC 20% rate (anonymous source), the February 27, 2026 joint statement, and The Verge piece itself. Present the 20% figure as unconfirmed.
- **confirmed**: 2024 reporting tied the contractual AGI trigger to about $100B in profit → About $100B in profits, reported by The Information and summarized by TechCrunch on 2024-12-26 (https://techcrunch.com/2024/12/26/microsoft-and-openai-have-a-financial-definition-of-agi-report/). Second-hand reporting of a private contract that neither company ever confirmed, as the researcher says. It should be labeled 'reported'.
- **confirmed**: DeepMind's Levels of AGI: performance x generality matrix; narrow superhuman AI exists; general AI is at 'Emerging' → L1 Emerging = equal to or somewhat better than an unskilled human. L2 Competent = at least 50th percentile of skilled adults. L3 Expert = at least 90th. L4 Exceptional (formerly Virtuoso) = at least 99th. L5 Superhuman = outperforms 100% of humans. Narrow L5: AlphaFold, AlphaZero, Stockfish. General L1: ChatGPT, Bard, Llama 2, Gemini. v1 2023-11-04; v5 2025-09-24; ICML 2024. (https://arxiv.org/html/2311.02462v5). Checked against the v5 HTML table and the arXiv version history. Thresholds and examples match. The 'Emerging' placement is the authors' assessment at the time of writing (2023, restated in v5), not a measurement.
- **confirmed**: Hendrycks et al. AGI score: GPT-4 27%, GPT-5 57%, with the stated per-domain profile → GPT-4: K8 RW6 M4 R0 WM2 MS0 MR4 V0 A0 S3 = 27%. GPT-5: K9 RW10 M10 R7 WM4 MS0 MR4 V4 A6 S3 = 57%. GPT-5 was tested in 'Auto' mode. Definition: "AGI is an AI that can match or exceed the cognitive versatility and proficiency of a well-educated adult." (https://arxiv.org/html/2510.18212v3). Table 1 re-fetched from the v3 HTML and all values match. This is a preprint (not peer reviewed), and the scores are the authors' own assessment against their own battery. I did not check the claim that agiranker.com shows 2026 scores around 80; leave it out.
- **confirmed**: ARC-AGI-3: GPT-6 Astra scored 62.7% (Standard) and 99.9% (Provider Adapter); ARC Prize declined to call it AGI → Standard harness, max effort: 62.7% at $26,098 (17.5% at low effort). Provider Adapter, high effort: 99.9% at $18,817 (max effort: 98.6% at $17,332). Fewer actions than the human baseline on 96.0% of levels, 51.7% fewer on average, under the Provider Adapter at max effort. Humans were paid $115 per 90-minute session plus $5 per completed game, about $12.78 per attempted game. Quote: "we are not claiming that it is AGI". Post by Greg Kamradt, 2026-09-03. (https://arcprize.org/blog/astra). Primary post fetched. Specify that the 96% action-efficiency figure is the Provider Adapter at MAX effort, while the 99.9% score is at HIGH effort. The leaderboard confirms ARC-AGI-2 at 95.0% and ARC-AGI-1 at 98.5%, but 98.5% is a six-way tie, so ARC-AGI-1 is fully saturated. Not verified: the launch-week scores (Gemini 3.1 Pro 0.37%, GPT-5.4 0.26%, Opus 4.6 0.25%), Chollet's '66%' tweet, the '2x faster' remark and 'ARC-AGI-4 in Q1 2027'. All are secondary; don't publish them without a primary source. ARC's own costs for Astra are listed as $26.1K across all three benchmarks, so cost-per-benchmark comparisons from the leaderboard are unreliable.
- **confirmed**: Altman: 'not a super useful term' (Aug 2025), then predicted an internal AGI system by year-end (TIME, Aug 26, 2026) → CNBC Squawk Box: "I think it's not a super useful term". The interview aired the week before CNBC's article dated 2025-08-11. TIME, 'Inside OpenAI's Reboot' (Alex Heath, 2026-08-26): "not quite yet" at AGI, and an internal system he would call AGI by year-end (TIME's paraphrase). Mark Chen: "80% of the way". (https://time.com/article/2026/08/26/openai-sam-altman-interview/). Both sources fetched. Minor date fix: the interview aired around the GPT-5 launch in early August 2025, and 2025-08-11 is the article date. The year-end prediction must be presented as TIME's paraphrase, not as a direct quote. Agree on dropping the Lex Fridman quote.
- **unsupported**: Brockman at the GPT-6 Astra launch: "not unreasonable to feel that we are now in the AGI era" → Not independently verified (https://www.fastcompany.com/91607269/openai-says-the-agi-era-has-begun-ai-researchers-arent-so-sure). Fast Company returned 403 to my fetch, the OpenAI primary page also returned 403 for the researcher, and my search budget ran out before I could find another source. The exact quote and the 'mission concept, not contractual trigger' framing come only from secondary sources. Do not quote verbatim until it is checked against the OpenAI launch post or a video/transcript.
- **confirmed**: Karpathy: economic definition of AGI; 'a decade away' → "AGI was a system you could go to that can do any economically valuable task at human performance or better"; "this is more accurately described as the decade of agents"; "they just don't work"; chapter 'AGI will blend into 2% GDP growth'. Dwarkesh Podcast, 2025-10-17. (https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/andrej-karpathy). All quotes are in the transcript. 'A decade away' is the episode title's phrasing; he frames it as the decade of agents.
- **confirmed**: Amodei avoids 'AGI' because of its 'sci-fi baggage and hype' → Footnote: AGI is "an imprecise term" that has "gathered a lot of sci-fi baggage and hype". Powerful AI is "smarter than a Nobel Prize winner across most relevant fields". Also uses "country of geniuses in a datacenter". Essay dated October 2024. (https://www.darioamodei.com/essay/machines-of-loving-grace). Confirmed in the 2024 essay. The claim that the 2026 'Adolescence of Technology' essay repeats this was not checked by the researcher or by me.
- **corrected**: BCG 'jagged frontier' experiment numbers → 758 consultants, about 7% of BCG's individual-contributor-level consultants (not of all its consultants). 12.2% more tasks completed, 25.1% faster, more than 40% higher quality. On the outside-frontier task, AI users were 19 percentage points less likely to produce correct solutions. (https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4573321). SSRN, HBS and INFORMS all returned 403 to me. The figures match the well-known abstract, but the abstract says the 7% is of individual-contributor-level consultants. I could not confirm the about 84% no-AI baseline (it comes from the paper body) or the Organization Science DOI 10.1287/orsc.2025.21838. Cite it as the HBS working paper 24-013 unless the journal version is confirmed.
- **confirmed**: METR 50% time horizon: Mythos Preview (early) at about 17.4h with a 95% CI of 8.5–55h, plus the other model values and doubling times → Mythos Preview (early, released 2026-04-07): p50 1044.78 min (508.88–3304.26), p80 185.91 min. Opus 4.6: 718.81 min (316.69–3633.79). Gemini 3.1 Pro: 384.15. GPT-5.4: 341.74. GPT-5: 203.01. GPT-4: 3.99. Doubling time since 2023: 128.74 days (CI 104.43–158.01); all-time, stitched: 187.78 days. (https://metr.org/assets/benchmark_results_1_1.yaml). YAML fetched and the values match exactly. The page confirms "Measurements above 16 hrs are unreliable with our current task suite", that it is "no longer actively updated" (last updated 2026-09-08), and that Mythos was added 2026-05-08. The YAML notes that the doubling-time fit EXCLUDES points with p50 above 16 h, so Mythos is not in the trend estimate; say so. I could not verify '5 of 228 tasks at 16h or more' or the system card's 34.4h figure. The page FAQ's GPT-5 'around 2 hours and 17 minutes' does differ from the v1.1 value of 203 min, so the warning about mixing suite versions is right.
- **confirmed**: METR's January 2026 note: time horizons are not delegation thresholds and do not generalize across domains → Error bars historically about 2x in each direction, wider for newer models (Opus 4.5: about 4h49m, CI 1h49m–20h25m). Visual computer-use horizons are 40–100x lower than math/software. A 50% horizon is not a delegation threshold, and some tasks need 98% or higher reliability. Doubling the horizon does not double automation. (https://metr.org/notes/2026-01-22-time-horizon-limitations/). Primary note fetched (Thomas Kwa, 2026-01-22). One precision fix: the note says visual computer use is 40–100x lower than math AND software/research tasks, which have similar horizons to each other.

### Missed evidence (from the fact-checker)

- The ARC Prize leaderboard (https://arcprize.org/leaderboard) shows models newer than Astra: GPT-6.1 Sol at 52.7% on ARC-AGI-3 Standard ($7.6K) and Gemini 4 Argon at 49.8% ($5.8K). Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Fable 5 also hit 98.5% on ARC-AGI-1, a six-way tie. The briefing should note that Astra is not the only frontier reference point, that cheaper models are close behind, and that ARC-AGI-1 is fully saturated.
- METR's time-horizon page is frozen (no longer actively updated as of 2026-09-08, https://metr.org/time-horizons/). Its doubling-time fit excludes any point with p50 above 16 h (benchmark_results_1_1.yaml), so the headline trend no longer covers the frontier. The briefing should say METR's continuous yardstick has itself saturated, which supports the 'end of measurement' theme.
- Claims in the research summary that were never put in the findings list, so neither the researcher nor I verified them: GDPval at about 85% for GPT-5.5 (vendor), GDPval at 47.6% for Opus 4.1, Remote Labor Index rising from 2.5% to 21.25% for Opus 5.5, Epoch ECI of about 166 for Astra, Astra's regression on Artificial Analysis's GDPval-AA, and Anthropic's observed-exposure figures (94% feasible vs 33% covered, no rise in unemployment). They are load-bearing for the summary's conclusions and need primary sources (openai.com GDPval paper, scale.com/leaderboard/rli, epoch.ai ECI, artificialanalysis.ai, Anthropic Economic Index) before publication.
- I could not check the Feb 27, 2026 Microsoft–OpenAI joint statement ("definition remains the same") or the CNBC 20% revenue-share figure. Find the primary statement or drop it.
- My web search budget ran out after the first query, so all checks above rely on direct fetches of primary pages. The Brockman quote, the launch-week ARC-AGI-3 scores and the Chollet tweet still need a dedicated search.

### Findings

- [high/primary] **OpenAI's charter defines AGI by economic output, not by cognition.**: "highly autonomous systems that outperform humans at most economically valuable work" (Charter published 2018-04-09; still live 2026). [OpenAI Charter](https://openai.com/charter/). The charter's wording, confirmed via search snippets of openai.com/charter. 'Most economically valuable work' is never put into numbers.
- [high/primary] **The October 2025 Microsoft–OpenAI agreement moved AGI verification to an independent expert panel. Before that, OpenAI's board could make the call alone.**: "Once AGI is declared by OpenAI, that declaration will now be verified by an independent expert panel." Microsoft's model/product IP rights run through 2032 and include post-AGI models. Research IP rights last until the panel verifies AGI or through 2030, whichever comes first. The revenue share stays in place until the panel verifies AGI. (2025-10-28). [The next chapter of the Microsoft–OpenAI partnership (Microsoft Official Blog)](https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2025/10/28/the-next-chapter-of-the-microsoft-openai-partnership/). Wording fetched directly from Microsoft's post. No public source names the panel's members, its selection process, or its criteria.
- [high/primary] **In April 2026 the companies amended the deal so OpenAI's payments to Microsoft no longer depend on AGI, which commentators read as the end of the AGI clause.**: Microsoft's license to OpenAI IP runs through 2032 and becomes non-exclusive. Microsoft stops paying OpenAI a revenue share. OpenAI's payments to Microsoft continue through 2030, "independent of OpenAI's technology progress," at the same percentage but subject to a total cap. CNBC cites an anonymous source for a 20% rate. (2026-04-27). [The next phase of the Microsoft-OpenAI partnership (Microsoft blog); CNBC; Simon Willison](https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2026/04/27/the-next-phase-of-the-microsoft-openai-partnership/). Microsoft's April 2026 post does not mention AGI or the expert panel at all. Calling the clause "dead" is interpretation by Simon Willison (simonwillison.net/2026/Apr/27/now-deceased-agi-clause/) and The Verge. CNBC: Microsoft "no longer has to decide how it responds" if OpenAI says it has reached AGI. A February 27, 2026 joint statement had said the contractual AGI definition 'remains the same'. Whether the panel survives the amendment is unclear.
- [medium/reputable_press] **In 2024, reporting said the contractual AGI trigger was tied to profit, not capability.**: AGI threshold reportedly about $100B in profits for OpenAI's earliest investors (2024-12-26). [TechCrunch, summarizing The Information](https://techcrunch.com/2024/12/26/microsoft-and-openai-have-a-financial-definition-of-agi-report/). Second-hand report of a private contract. Never confirmed by either company.
- [high/peer_reviewed] **DeepMind's 'Levels of AGI' rates systems on performance (percentiles of skilled adults) crossed with generality (narrow vs. general). Under it, narrow superhuman AI already exists and general AI was 'Emerging' as of 2023.**: Level 1 Emerging means equal to or somewhat better than an unskilled human. Level 2 Competent is at least the 50th percentile of skilled adults, Level 3 Expert at least the 90th, Level 4 Virtuoso/'Exceptional' at least the 99th, and Level 5 Superhuman beats 100% of humans. Narrow Level 5 examples: AlphaFold, AlphaZero, Stockfish. General Level 1 examples: ChatGPT, Bard, Llama 2, Gemini. (v1 2023-11-04; ICML 2024; v5 2025-09-24 renamed Level 4 'Exceptional'). [Morris et al., Levels of AGI for Operationalizing Progress on the Path to AGI (arXiv 2311.02462)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.02462). Version history and rename checked on arXiv. The level thresholds and examples come from summaries of the paper's table; check the PDF table before quoting. Even this framework is human-relative, built on percentiles of skilled humans.
- [high/preprint] **Hendrycks et al. score AGI as a percentage across ten equally weighted psychometric domains. GPT-4 scores 27% and GPT-5 scores 57%.**: GPT-4 = 27%, GPT-5 = 57%. GPT-5 per domain, out of 10 each: General Knowledge 9, Reading/Writing 10, Math 10, On-the-spot Reasoning 7, Working Memory 4, Long-term Memory Storage 0, Long-term Memory Retrieval 4, Visual 4, Auditory 6, Speed 3. GPT-4: K 8, RW 6, M 4, R 0, WM 2, MS 0, MR 4, V 0, A 0, S 3. GPT-5 was tested in 'Auto' mode. (arXiv v1 2025-10-21; v3 2025-12-03). [Hendrycks et al., A Definition of AGI (arXiv 2510.18212)](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.18212v3). Table 1 fetched from the arXiv HTML. 33 authors. AGI is defined as matching 'the cognitive versatility and proficiency of a well-educated adult' (Cattell-Horn-Carroll theory). The authors themselves advise reporting the cognitive profile, not only the single number. The official sites (agidefinition.ai and agidefinition.safe.ai) list only GPT-4 and GPT-5, with no 2026 model scores. agiranker.com shows scores around 80 but is not affiliated.
- [high/official_leaderboard] **ARC Prize defines AGI as human-level learning efficiency. GPT-6 Astra nearly solved ARC-AGI-3 with a provider-specific harness, but ARC Prize explicitly declined to call it AGI.**: ARC-AGI-3 Semi-Private: 62.7% with the Standard harness (max effort, about $26K total) and 99.9% with OpenAI's Provider Adapter harness (high effort, about $18.8K total). Astra used fewer actions than the human baseline on 96.0% of levels, 51.7% fewer per level on average. Humans were paid about $12.78 per attempted game. Frontier models at the March 25, 2026 launch scored under 1%: Gemini 3.1 Pro 0.37%, GPT-5.4 0.26%, Opus 4.6 0.25%. (2026-09-03 (launch-week numbers from 2026-03-25)). [ARC Prize blog: OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra on ARC-AGI-3 (Greg Kamradt)](https://arcprize.org/blog/astra). The post says "we are not claiming that it is AGI." ARC's definition: "AGI is a system that can match the learning efficiency of humans" (arcprize.org/arc-agi). Chollet tweeted 66% on the standard harness, versus 62.7% in the table. He also said progress came about 2x faster than he expected, and that ARC-AGI-4 is due Q1 2027. Launch-week scores are from secondary coverage. The leaderboard also shows ARC-AGI-2 at 95.0% (Astra) and ARC-AGI-1 at 98.5%, so both are close to saturated.
- [high/reputable_press] **Altman said in August 2025 that AGI is 'not a super useful term,' then in August 2026 predicted OpenAI would have an internal system he would call AGI by year-end.**: Aug 2025, CNBC Squawk Box: "I think it's not a super useful term." Aug 26, 2026, TIME: OpenAI is "not quite yet" at AGI, and by year-end will have an internal system he would call AGI (TIME's paraphrase). Mark Chen: OpenAI is "80% of the way" there. (2025-08-11 (CNBC); 2026-08-26 (TIME)). [TIME, 'Inside OpenAI's Reboot'; CNBC via secondary reproductions](https://time.com/article/2026/08/26/openai-sam-altman-interview/). The TIME article was fetched and quotes checked. I did not fetch the original CNBC article; the quote is consistent across Fortune and other reproductions. A claimed March 2026 Lex Fridman quote, "I think we've achieved AGI," is unverified; don't use it.
- [medium/reputable_press] **At the GPT-6 Astra launch, OpenAI's president suggested the 'AGI era' had begun, while describing AGI as a mission concept rather than a contractual trigger.**: Brockman: "not unreasonable to feel that we are now in the AGI era" (2026-09-03). [Secondary coverage (Betanews, Sify, Fast Company headline 'OpenAI says the AGI era has begun. AI researchers aren't so sure')](https://www.fastcompany.com/91607269/openai-says-the-agi-era-has-begun-ai-researchers-arent-so-sure). The primary OpenAI page (openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/) returned 403 and could not be checked. The exact quote and the 'mission concept' framing come only from secondary sources, so verify before quoting.
- [high/primary] **Karpathy's definition of AGI is economic, and he said AGI is about a decade away.**: "AGI was a system you could go to that can do any economically valuable task at human performance or better." Also: "this is more accurately described as the decade of agents" and "they just don't work." Chapter title: 'AGI will blend into 2% GDP growth.' (2025-10-17). [Dwarkesh Podcast: Andrej Karpathy — AGI is still a decade away](https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/andrej-karpathy). Quotes taken from the transcript.
- [high/primary] **Amodei avoids the term AGI and uses 'powerful AI' / 'a country of geniuses in a datacenter' instead.**: AGI is "an imprecise term that has gathered a lot of sci-fi baggage and hype" (footnote). Powerful AI is "smarter than a Nobel Prize winner across most relevant fields." (2024-10 (Machines of Loving Grace); reiterated in 'The Adolescence of Technology' (2026)). [Dario Amodei, Machines of Loving Grace](https://www.darioamodei.com/essay/machines-of-loving-grace). Quotes taken from the essay. The 2026 essay was not fetched.
- [low/secondary_aggregator] **Hassabis describes current systems as 'jagged intelligences' that lack continual learning, long-term planning and consistent reasoning, and his stated AGI timeline has moved during 2026.**: Feb 2026 (India AI Impact Summit): AGI maybe 5–8 years away. Around May 2026 (Axios): about 2030. Oct 2026 (WSJ, per Techmeme): 2–3 years. (2026-02 to 2026-10). [Storyboard18 and other secondary coverage](https://www.storyboard18.com/brand-makers/google-deepmind-ceo-says-agi-not-here-yet-calls-current-ai-jagged-intelligence-90028.htm). No primary transcript checked, and the WSJ 2–3 year figure is unconfirmed. Use only as illustration with hedging, or find the primary source.
- [medium/peer_reviewed] **The 'jagged frontier' field experiment at BCG: GPT-4 helped strongly on tasks inside its capability and hurt on a task just outside it.**: 758 consultants (about 7% of BCG's consultant workforce). Inside the frontier: 12.2% more tasks completed, 25.1% faster, more than 40% higher quality. Outside-frontier task: AI users were 19 percentage points less likely to get it right; the no-AI baseline was about 84% correct. (HBS working paper 2023-09; Organization Science version (DOI 10.1287/orsc.2025.21838)). [Dell'Acqua, McFowland, Mollick, Lifshitz-Assaf, Kellogg, Rajendran, Krayer, Candelon, Lakhani — Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier](https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4573321). SSRN and HBS pages returned 403. The numbers are consistent across several secondary summaries and match the well-known abstract, but check the abstract before publishing. Limits: one firm, GPT-4 (2023), consulting tasks only.
- [high/primary] **METR's 50% time horizon has risen from minutes to more than half a working day. The top 2026 measurement is beyond what METR says it can measure reliably.**: Time Horizon 1.1, 50% horizon (95% CI): Claude Mythos Preview (early, released 2026-04-07) 1,044.8 min ≈ 17.4 h (508.9–3,304.3 min ≈ 8.5–55 h); 80% horizon 185.9 min ≈ 3.1 h. Claude Opus 4.6: 718.8 min ≈ 12.0 h (316.7–3,633.8). Gemini 3.1 Pro: 384.1 min ≈ 6.4 h. GPT-5.4: 341.7 min ≈ 5.7 h. GPT-5: 203 min ≈ 3.4 h. GPT-4 (2023): 4.0 min. Doubling time since 2023 about 129 days (CI about 104–158); about 188 days across all time. (Mythos added 2026-05-08; page no longer actively updated as of 2026-09-08). [METR Time Horizons page and benchmark_results_1_1.yaml](https://metr.org/time-horizons/). METR: "Measurements above 16 hrs are unreliable with our current task suite." The suite has only 5 tasks of 16 h or more out of 228. The GPT-5 value on v1.1 (about 3.4 h) differs from the page text's 'around 2 hours and 17 minutes', which is likely the v1.0 figure; don't mix suite versions. Models released after May 2026 (Opus 4.8, Fable 5, Astra, Opus 5.5) are not measured. Anthropic's Mythos system card reported an imputed 34.4 h; that is a vendor figure, not METR's.
- [high/primary] **METR itself warns that time horizons are not a delegation threshold and do not generalize across domains.**: Error bars about 2x in each direction. Visual computer-use horizons are 40–100x lower than math. A 50% horizon does not mean tasks under that length can be delegated, since some need 98%+ reliability. Doubling the horizon does not double automation. (2026-01-22). [METR, Clarifying limitations of time horizon](https://metr.org/notes/2026-01-22-time-horizon-limitations/). Essential caveat for a general audience.
- [high/preprint] **In the original GDPval, the best model's deliverables were judged as good as or better than industry experts' in under half of tasks.**: 1,320 tasks, 44 occupations, 9 sectors; experts average 14 years' experience; 220-task gold subset. Claude Opus 4.1: 47.6% wins or ties. GPT-5 (high): 39.0% wins. o3: 35.2%. GPT-4o: 12.5% wins. Blinded pairwise grading by occupation experts. Naive GPT-5 speed and cost ratios were about 90x and 474x, but with a 'try once, then fix it yourself' workflow only about 1.12x faster and 1.18x cheaper. (2025-10-05 (arXiv v1; announced 2025-09-25)). [GDPval: Evaluating AI Model Performance on Real-World Economically Valuable Tasks (arXiv 2510.04374)](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.04374). Paper's own limitations: tasks are one-shot and precisely specified, not interactive, with no tacit knowledge or physical work. The automated grader agrees with humans 66% of the time, versus 71% between human raters.
- [medium/vendor_reported] **OpenAI reports a GDPval wins-or-ties rate of about 85% for GPT-5.5.**: GPT-5.5 84.9% wins or ties vs. professionals across 44 occupations. In the same table: GPT-5.4 83.0%, GPT-5.5 Pro 82.3%, Claude Opus 4.7 80.3%, Gemini 3.1 Pro 67.3%. (2026-04-23 (GPT-5.5 release, per secondary)). [OpenAI, Introducing GPT-5.5 (via search excerpt)](https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-5-5/). The page returned 403 and the numbers come from a search-result summary, so recheck the table. Vendor-run eval with no independent replication found. No GDPval figure was found for GPT-6 Astra in OpenAI's launch materials.
- [high/secondary_aggregator] **On Artificial Analysis's independent GDPval-AA, GPT-6 Astra scored lower than its predecessor: a newer 'smarter' model was worse at simulated professional work.**: Astra about 45 Elo points below GPT-5.6 Sol on GDPval-AA v2, attributed to fewer agent turns (24 vs. 45). Intelligence Index: Astra (max) 53, tied with Claude Fable 5.1 and 6 points above GPT-5.6 Sol. Terminal-Bench v4.0: Astra 59%, Fable 5.1 52%, GPT-5.6 Sol 40%. (2026-09-09). [Artificial Analysis, Benchmarking GPT-6 Astra](https://artificialanalysis.ai/articles/benchmarking-gpt-6-astra). Independent third-party evaluator. GDPval-AA uses an LLM grader and Elo, so it is not comparable with OpenAI's expert-graded GDPval.
- [high/official_leaderboard] **The Remote Labor Index (real paid freelance projects judged by humans) shows rapid growth from a low base. Even the best agent fails most projects.**: Automation rate = share of projects scored 2 or 3 (as good as or better than a human professional's deliverable). Current leaderboard: Opus 5.5 21.25%, GPT-6 Astra 20.83%, Fable 5.1 17.92%, Gemini Argon 4 17.08%, Fable 5 15.83%, Opus 4.8 8.33%, GPT-5.5 (Codex) 6.25%, GPT-5.2 2.50%, GPT-5 1.67%. Best at launch: 2.5% (Manus). (Leaderboard accessed 2026-11-07 (no dates on page); RLI launched 2025-10). [Scale Labs Remote Labor Index leaderboard](https://labs.scale.com/leaderboard/rli). Run jointly by Scale and CAIS. The leaderboard shows no per-entry dates.
- [high/primary] **CAIS's July 2026 RLI update found that automated LLM judges overstate how well the newest models do on real work by about 2.3–2.9x.**: Fable 5 15.8%, Opus 4.8 8.3%, GPT-5.5 6.25% (human-judged). LLM judge: GPT-5.5 17.9% vs. 6.25% human-judged (2.9x); Opus 4.8 18.8% vs. 8.33% (2.3x). The judge still ranks models correctly (Spearman ρ = 0.90). RLI pass rates do not fall as human completion time rises, so time-horizon analysis does not fit RLI. No Fable 5 deliverable would be accepted as finished professional work. (2026-07-01). [CAIS, A Significant Increase in Digital Labor Automation](https://safe.ai/blog/significant-increase-in-digital-labor-automation). Strong evidence that LLM-graded 'economic value' evals can mislead.
- [high/primary] **The Epoch Capabilities Index (ECI) combines many benchmarks into one open-ended scale. The top models are statistically tied, and scores shift as results come in.**: Scale anchored at Claude 3.5 Sonnet = 130 and GPT-5 = 150; no maximum. As of 2026-09-13: GPT-6 Astra 166.31 (90% CI 163.00–171.88), Claude Fable 5.1 164.47, GPT-5.6 Sol 161.81, Kimi K3 157.63. Astra launched at 169.23 and fell to 166.31 as more results arrived. Math-ECI: Astra 169.83 vs. Fable 5.1 165.73. SWE-ECI: Fable 5.1 167.44 vs. Astra 163.58. (2026-09-16). [Epoch AI, GPT-6 Astra leads on math benchmarks, but not on software engineering; Epoch ECI page](https://epoch.ai/data-insights/astra-eci-breakdown). Later October 2026 figures (Opus 5.5 168.83 vs. Astra 168.81; Astra 169.1) appear only in secondary and prediction-market sources and could not be checked. The live leaderboard didn't render; the CSV was updated Nov 4 and Nov 7, 2026.
- [high/primary] **Anthropic's 'observed exposure' measure finds actual AI use is far below what is theoretically feasible, and finds no systematic rise in unemployment among exposed workers.**: Computer & Math: about 94% of tasks theoretically feasible for LLMs vs. about 33% actually covered by Claude. Office & Admin: about 90% feasible. Computer programmers 75% coverage, data entry keyers 67%. About 30% of workers have zero coverage. No significant unemployment increase for highly exposed workers since late 2022. Job-finding rate for 22–25 year-olds in exposed occupations is down about 14% relative to 2022, barely significant. BLS projects about 0.6 percentage points less growth per 10-point increase in coverage. (2026-03-05). [Anthropic, Labor market impacts of AI: A new measure (Massenkoff & McCrory)](https://www.anthropic.com/research/labor-market-impacts). Based on usage of one vendor's platform (Claude), so it understates total AI use. Fully automated use gets full weight and augmentative use half weight.
- [medium/primary] **Anthropic Economic Index 'Learning curves' report: usage is spreading out and the earlier '49% of jobs' figure barely changed.**: The top 10 O*NET tasks fell from 24% to 19% of Claude.ai conversations (Nov 2025 to Feb 2026). About 49% of jobs have at least 25% of tasks performed using Claude (Nov 2025 report; 'barely changed'). Average wage-value of tasks fell from $49.3 to $47.9 per hour. In the Jan 2026 report on Claude.ai, augmentation was 52% vs. automation 45%. (2026-03-24 (sample 2026-02-05 to 02-12)). [Anthropic Economic Index report: Learning curves](https://www.anthropic.com/research/economic-index-march-2026-report). The 52/45 split comes from secondary summaries of the January 2026 report and was not checked on Anthropic's page. The economic-index hub says 'last updated June 26, 2026', but the newer edition's contents were not retrieved.
- [medium/primary] **Anthropic research suggests robots could technically do most physical tasks but are cost-competitive for very few, a reminder that 'can do' and 'economically does' are different yardsticks.**: About three-quarters of physical tasks technically doable; cost-competitive for 0.3% (2026-09-30). [Anthropic, What work can robots do?](https://www.anthropic.com/research). Headline read from Anthropic's research index page only; the article itself was not fetched.
- [low/secondary_aggregator] **OpenAI introduced 'LifeVal', an everyday-errands benchmark, and its own chart shows gains flattening.**: GPT-5.2 49.3%, GPT-5.4 73.0%, GPT-5.6 80.0%, GPT-6 Astra 80.6% (xhigh; unweighted average reward) (2026-11-02). [Secondary coverage (aiweekly, DailyMech, FourWeekMBA)](https://dailymech.com/articles/openai-lifeval-everyday-tasks-benchmark.html). Only OpenAI models are scored, and the task set and grading are reportedly unreleased. Vendor-made benchmark with no independent check. Usable as an example of labs creating their own yardsticks, not as evidence of capability.

### surprises

- The contractual AGI trigger between Microsoft and OpenAI, once reportedly tied to about $100B in profits and later to an expert panel, was in effect dropped in April 2026: OpenAI's payments to Microsoft now run through 2030 'independent of OpenAI's technology progress.'
- OpenAI's CEO called AGI 'not a super useful term' in August 2025, then a year later predicted an internal system he would 'call AGI' by the end of 2026.
- ARC-AGI-3 went from under 1% for every frontier model at launch (March 2026) to 62.7% standard and 99.9% with a custom harness six months later. Under that harness, Astra used fewer moves than humans on 96% of levels, yet the benchmark's creators still say it isn't AGI.
- In the Hendrycks AGI score, GPT-5 gets a perfect 10/10 on math and on reading and writing but 0/10 on forming new long-term memories, the same as GPT-4.
- METR's best AI agent can, half the time, finish software tasks that take a skilled human about 17 hours, but METR's own test suite has only 5 tasks that long, so it can barely measure this.
- A newer flagship (GPT-6 Astra) scored worse than its predecessor on an independent test of professional work deliverables.
- AI judges grading AI work overstated the newest models' real freelance success by nearly 3x compared with human graders.
- Anthropic estimates robots could technically do about three-quarters of physical tasks but are cost-competitive for only 0.3%.

### counterevidence

- Against 'AGI is useless as a yardstick': Hendrycks et al. and DeepMind's Levels of AGI both turn 'human-level' into measurable profiles, and the authors themselves say to report the domain profile. Human-relative measurement can work if it is broken down by domain. The real problem is the single yes/no label, not human comparison as such.
- Almost every proposed 'better' yardstick still uses humans as the reference point: GDPval (expert deliverables), RLI (professional freelancers), METR (human completion time), ARC-AGI-3 (human action efficiency). The defensible claim is 'human-level is useful task by task but misleading as one finish line.'
- Against belief (1) on speed: on the most realistic work benchmark (RLI), the best agent completes about 21% of paid projects at acceptable quality, and CAIS says no Fable 5 deliverable would be accepted as finished professional work.
- GPT-6 Astra scored about 45 Elo lower than GPT-5.6 Sol on GDPval-AA v2 and lower on presentation quality. Newer frontier models do not get better at everything.
- OpenAI's own LifeVal chart shows gains slowing: 73.0 to 80.0 to 80.6.
- Anthropic finds no systematic rise in unemployment among highly exposed workers since late 2022, and actual coverage is about 33% vs. about 94% feasible in Computer & Math. Capability is running well ahead of economic impact.
- On belief (1) and METR: the top time horizon (Mythos early, about 17.4 h) has a 95% CI of about 8.5–55 h, and METR says values above 16 h are unreliable. The fastest-looking recent points are the least certain. METR's page stopped active updates in September 2026, and post-May models are unmeasured.
- LLM-graded benchmarks overstate real-work performance on the newest models by about 2.3–2.9x (CAIS RLI analysis). Vendor-reported GDPval numbers like 84.9% for GPT-5.5 should be treated with care.
- ARC-AGI-3 results depend heavily on the harness: 62.7% standard vs. 99.9% with OpenAI's adapter, so a scaffolding choice can make a model look superhuman or not. ARC Prize and Chollet explicitly decline to call Astra AGI.
- Against belief (2): models still score 0 on long-term memory storage in Hendrycks et al. (GPT-5), and METR finds visual computer-use horizons 40–100x shorter than math. Superhuman performance is narrow and patchy.
- The GDPval paper's own speed and cost analysis: naive gains of about 90x faster and 474x cheaper shrink to about 1.1–1.4x faster and 1.2–1.6x cheaper once human review and fixing are counted.

### chart_ideas

- METR 50% time horizon over time, log scale: GPT-2 (2019) 0.05 min to GPT-4 (2023) 4 min to GPT-5 (2025) 203 min to Claude Mythos Preview early (2026-04) 1,045 min, with 95% CI bars and a shaded 'unreliable above 16 h' band. Mark v1.0 points separately. Data: metr.org/assets/benchmark_results_1_1.yaml.
- Remote Labor Index automation rate by model/date: 2.5% at launch (Oct 2025), 4.17% (Opus 4.6 Cowork), 8.33% (Opus 4.8), 15.83% (Fable 5), 21.25% (Opus 5.5), with the remaining about 79% shown as 'not yet automated'. Data: labs.scale.com/leaderboard/rli and safe.ai blog (July 1, 2026).
- Radar or small-multiple bars of the Hendrycks AGI profile, GPT-4 vs. GPT-5, across 10 domains, showing jaggedness (math 10, memory storage 0). Data: arXiv 2510.18212 Table 1.
- Theoretical vs. observed AI exposure by occupational category, e.g. Computer & Math 94% vs. 33%. Data: anthropic.com/research/labor-market-impacts (full category values are in the figure; fetch the data files).
- ARC-AGI-3 harness gap: launch scores (<1%) vs. Astra standard 62.7% vs. Astra adapter 99.9%, labeled with cost. Data: arcprize.org/blog/astra and the leaderboard.
- Human-graded vs. LLM-graded RLI rates for GPT-5.5 (6.25% vs. 17.9%) and Opus 4.8 (8.33% vs. 18.8%) as a 'measurement inflates' chart. Data: safe.ai blog July 2026.
- Timeline of AGI definitions and contract changes: 2018 charter; Nov 2023 Levels of AGI; Dec 2024 $100B profit report; Aug 2025 Altman 'not useful'; Oct 2025 expert panel and Hendrycks definition; Apr 2026 clause dropped; Aug 2026 Altman 'AGI by year end'; Sep 2026 Astra 'AGI era'. Sources as cited.
- GDPval wins-or-ties trajectory: GPT-4o 12.5% (wins only) and Claude Opus 4.1 47.6% (Sept 2025, paper) to GPT-5.5 84.9% (OpenAI, Apr 2026, vendor-reported). Label vendor vs. paper values clearly, and note that wins-only and wins-or-ties are not directly comparable.

### open_questions

- Did the expert-panel AGI verification mechanism survive the April 27, 2026 amendment? Microsoft's post is silent. Check OpenAI's page openai.com/index/next-phase-of-microsoft-partnership/ (it returned 403 here) and The Verge article.
- Exact wording of OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra launch claims about the 'AGI era' and Brockman's quote. openai.com returned 403, so only secondary sources were seen.
- Exact GDPval scores for GPT-5.2, GPT-5.4, GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6. The GPT-5.5 table (84.9%) came via a search summary. I could not check other versions: the search budget ran out before I could look up GPT-5.2's reported 70.9%.
- Have Hendrycks et al. or CAIS published AGI-definition scores for any 2026 model? None were found on agidefinition.ai or the arXiv versions (latest v3, Dec 3, 2025).
- Dell'Acqua et al. exact numbers should be checked against the abstract or the Organization Science version. SSRN and HBS returned 403.
- Current ECI leader as of November 2026. Epoch's live leaderboard didn't render, and the October figures (Opus 5.5 vs. Astra about 168.8) are unverified.
- METR time horizons for models after May 2026 (Opus 4.8, Fable 5/5.1, GPT-5.6, Astra, Opus 5.5): METR's page stopped active updates. Has METR released a new task suite (TH 2.0) or a Frontier Risk Report update? The May 19, 2026 Frontier Risk Report figures (about 12 h public frontier) were seen only via mirrors.
- Hassabis's 2026 statements (WSJ '2–3 years', a July 2026 essay definition) still need primary sources.
- Contents of the June 26, 2026 Anthropic Economic Index edition ('Cadences'?) were not retrieved.
- Note: the web search budget ran out partway through, so remaining checks relied on direct page fetches. RSI and weight-editing news (belief 4) was outside this topic and not researched here.

## rsi

As of 2026-11-07 the evidence says AI is now speeding up AI research in a measurable way. It also says no lab, by its own definitions, has a model that improves itself on its own, and most of the strongest numbers are self-reported by the labs. The best independent data point is METR's assessment of Anthropic, published 2026-11-05. METR estimates Anthropic is improving model capabilities on well-specified tasks at about twice its March 2024 to October 2025 rate (1.9x, 95% CI 1.5-2.8x). Most METR staff guess about 80% of that speed-up comes from AI. METR also says Anthropic redacted much of the most informative evidence, so readers must rely on METR's judgment. Labs' own claims are bolder:
- OpenAI declared on 2026-09-06 that it had met its 'automated research intern' goal. Its headline metric, 3.1 agent-workdays per human workday, counts how long agents run, not the quality of what they produce. Over half of successful 4-8 hour tasks still needed a human to step in.
- Anthropic's 'When AI builds itself' essay (June 2026) says Claude wrote more than 80% of code merged at Anthropic in May 2026. The authors themselves call their 8x code-output figure 'almost certainly an overstatement'.
- Anthropic's amended IPO filing (S-1/A, 2026-11-02) says it 'may imminently meet' its own threshold for AI-driven acceleration, while stating no current model exceeds it.

Formal safety evaluations still come out below threshold. GPT-6 Astra does not reach OpenAI's 'High' level for AI self-improvement. Claude Opus 5.5 scores 55.8% on Anthropic's internal CoBench 2.1 test, against roughly 85% that Anthropic says a model able to replace its research staff would reach. The author's belief (4) needs a correction. The widely shared 'models editing their own weights' story (MIT CSAIL, 2026-11-02) is an unreviewed blog post. In it GPT-6 Astra edited a different, tiny model (Qwen3.5-0.8B) to stop it getting stuck in reasoning loops. Astra did not edit its own weights, and the authors say the edit 'could not add an ability the model lacked'. Google's AlphaEvolve shows real but modest AI-for-AI gains: about a 1% cut in Gemini training time (2025), and a circuit it proposed was put into next-generation TPU chips. Skeptics argue that compute, experiment time and research judgment remain the limits. Narayanan, Marcus and Toner say measurement is hard and lab claims double as marketing. Epoch's economic models suggest compute bottlenecks could prevent a runaway. For the author: belief (4) holds in a weaker form. 'AI is substantially speeding up AI development, with humans still steering' is supported. 'AI is improving itself on its own' is not, by anyone's measure, including the labs'. Note: I used up the web-search budget before I could check a few items. They are listed in open_questions; a follow-up message can continue the search.

### Fact-check verdict

Of the 14 findings, 7 are confirmed as stated (most of them against METR, OpenAI and press sources), 4 need corrections and 3 could not be verified. The summary's headline story is sound. The core independent claim, METR's Nov 5 numbers (1.9x [1.5-2.8x], the March 2024 to Oct 2025 baseline, 80% attributed to AI, the redaction and independent-review caveats), checks out exactly against the primary source. So do the GPT-6 Astra 'below High' finding, the S-1/A quotes (via TechCrunch) and the Anthropic Institute essay figures.

Required corrections:
1. The RSP v3.4 'split into entry-level automation and effective-scaling acceleration' is wrong. That split was made in v2.1 (March 2025). What v3.4 actually says is that the threshold is meant to capture 'the onset of dramatic recursive self-improvement' and 'has proven difficult to operationalize'. That is a better quote for this piece anyway.
2. The 5-61h time-horizon bracket has no stated confidence level, so it should not be called a 95% range.
3. The essay's survey covered 130 research staff, not 130 employees. Its 'June 2026' publication date is not shown on the page.

Treat as unverified or vendor-reported:
- All of OpenAI's Sept 6 figures (3.1 agent-workdays, spend, interventions). Both my fetch and the researcher's got 403s.
- The Opus 5.5 CoBench numbers and the Haiku 5.5 AECI point scores. Both PDFs are over 10MB, so these rest on mirrors and snippets.
- The Mythos slope ratios and the 'lower confidence' wording. These are not in the cited LessWrong source.
- The '~1.5x preliminary METR estimate'. It appears nowhere in METR's report.
- The Altman 'March 2028' and 'may totally fail' wording. It matches the known X post, but I could not fetch it, and TechCrunch does not have it.

Limits of this check: the shared web-search budget ran out on my first search, so I worked only from direct fetches of the cited URLs. I could not independently check the summary-only items: the MIT CSAIL weight-editing blog, AlphaEvolve's roughly 1% Gemini training-time cut and the TPU circuit, Narayanan/Marcus/Toner, and the Epoch bottleneck models. The AlphaEvolve figures match DeepMind's May 2025 announcement as I know it, but I did not re-fetch it. A follow-up message can continue the search.

- **confirmed**: OpenAI publicly set internal goals of an automated AI research intern by September 2026 and a true automated AI researcher by March 2028 (Altman X post, Oct 28, 2025). → Intern by September 2026 'running on hundreds of thousands of GPUs'; 'true automated AI researcher by March of 2028'; 'We may totally fail at this goal'. Posted Oct 28, 2025 (US time), after the livestream. (https://x.com/sama/status/1983584366547829073). x.com returned 402 to my fetcher, so I could not read the post in this session. The wording matches the widely reported text of the post. TechCrunch (Oct 28, 2025) gives only 'an intern-level research assistant by September 2026' and 'legitimate AI researcher by 2028'. It does not have 'March 2028', the GPU phrase or the 'may totally fail' quote. Cite the X post as the source for those, not TechCrunch.
- **unsupported**: OpenAI's Sept 6, 2026 post says it met its automated research intern goal: 3.1 agent-workdays per human workday, >$600/day median inference, >$7,000/day 90th percentile, over half of successful 4-8h tasks needed human intervention. → Not independently verified. The OpenAI page returned 403 to me as well. TNW (Sept 29, 2026) confirms only that Altman said the goal was reached 'a few weeks' before DevDay. (https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/). Every number here comes from secondary summaries (Help Net Security, CellCog), not the primary post. Label it vendor-reported and second-hand. A different OpenAI framing seems to pull the other way: at DevDay, Tejal Patwardhan said models complete 'more than a third of day-long research tasks without human help' (per TNW). That is a different task bucket from 'over half of successful 4-8h tasks needed intervention', so do not merge the two. Keep the '4x speedup' figure out of the briefing.
- **confirmed**: Altman said on stage at DevDay (2026-09-29): 'We now have an AI research intern.' → Quote confirmed. DevDay keynote, San Francisco, Tuesday Sept 29, 2026. Altman said the goal had been reached a few weeks earlier, against a 12-month prediction made the year before. (https://thenextweb.com/news/sam-altman-openai-ai-research-intern-devday-keynote). This confirms the claim was made, not that it is true. TNW also quotes Patwardhan: models complete more than a third of day-long research tasks without human help.
- **confirmed**: GPT-6 Astra does not reach OpenAI's Preparedness 'High' threshold for AI Self-Improvement, though it is the first model rated Critical for cybersecurity; Research Debugging 78.05%; NanoGPT best human 72.38%. → Section 10 summary: Astra does not reach High for AI Self-Improvement. Internal Research Debugging score is 78.05% (41 real bugs plus 6 alignment-auditing tasks), below the 'indicative threshold for High capability'. NanoGPT best human solution is 72.38%. The card says: 'Astra is our first model to reach the Critical level of cybersecurity capability.' Published Sept 3, 2026; change log runs to Oct 30, 2026. (https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra). The card gives no number for the High threshold, only 'indicative threshold'. KernelGen 1P, PostTrainBench Lite and MLE-Bench Revised have no numeric scores in the text. I did not check the claim that the GPT-6.1 Sol addendum is also below High.
- **confirmed**: METR (2026-11-05) finds Anthropic's AECI growth since Oct 2025 is 1.9x the prior rate [95% CI 1.5-2.8x], ~+15 to ~+29 pts/yr; robustness 1.1x-2.9x; 1.4x for all developers; ~80% attributed to AI by staff; confident not due to training compute. → 1.9x [1.5-2.8x]. Breakpoint Oct 7, 2025. Baseline period March 2024 to Oct 2025, rising from about +15 to about +29 pts/yr. Robustness 1.1x (cloglog) to 2.9x (log-log). All developers 1.4x (+17 to +24 pts/yr). Staff guess about 80% is due to AI. Quote: 'We are confident that the acceleration was not due to acceleration in training compute.' Applies to 'well-specified tasks'. 'Signs of life' for autonomous contributions. (https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/). Caveats confirmed. Anthropic redacted most of the evidence METR found most informative. There was no sampling access to non-public models (AEF-1 item 1.1.1 = No). Item 4.1 (enough methodological detail for independent review) = No, along with 1.1, 3.3, 4.2, 4.6 and 5.4. Claude was involved in most of the data collection and analysis. The 'significant risk ... even faster acceleration' quote is confirmed, and the term RSI does not appear. The compute statement cannot be checked because its evidence was redacted. METR also says the late-2025 acceleration was probably influenced by non-AI factors.
- **confirmed**: Anthropic staff self-reported AI speedup rose from ~1.2x (Claude 3.7 Sonnet, Jan 2025) to ~12x (Model 4, Oct 2026) via Slack polls; interviews centered around 2x. → About 1.2x to about 12x, with an exponential fit of about 3.9x/yr. METR calls this 'very loose evidence'. The interview impression was a wide range centered around 2x. METR's tally of researchers' quotes on whether they support >2x: 2 yes, 3 lean yes, 4 unclear, 0 lean no, 2 no. (https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/). The tally adds up to 11 even though the text says 12 researchers. This is a small inconsistency in METR's own report and does not need correcting here.
- **unsupported**: Anthropic's Haiku 5.5 system card reported 1.7x AECI acceleration vs METR's 1.9x (gap mainly from internal-only models); AECI scores Haiku 5.5 167.11, Opus 5.5 174.56. → The 1.7x figure and the explanation for the gap ('the inclusion of Anthropic's recent internal-only models') are confirmed via METR. The scores 167.11 and 174.56 are not in the METR report, and I could not open the card (PDF over 10MB). (https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/). Cite the 1.7x to METR. Drop the AECI point scores or mark them unverified. METR calls AECI 'Anthropic's version of the Epoch Capabilities Index', estimated with Epoch's public code. It does not use the word 'fork'. The only AECI score METR cites is Model 4's (178.6, or 179.7 after dropping three benchmarks).
- **unsupported**: Claude Opus 5.5 system card: CoBench 2.1 Opus 5.5 55.8%, Mythos 5.1 53.4%, Opus 5 53.2% vs ~85% for a full staff substitute; 'remains far from substituting for our research scientists and engineers'. → Could not verify. The PDF is over 10MB and could not be fetched. METR's report does not mention CoBench. (https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/fc1b44717c85dc068bc6ba5024219938094694bd/Claude%20Opus%205.5%20System%20Card.pdf). The numbers come from search snippets and unofficial mirrors (malob.github.io, GitHub), so they are secondary. The '~1.5x preliminary METR estimate' supposedly cited in the card does not appear anywhere in METR's Nov 5 report. METR mentions the Opus 5.5 card only for token-expenditure curves. Do not present 1.5x as METR's estimate.
- **unsupported**: Claude Mythos Preview system card (Apr 7, 2026) concluded it did not cross the AI R&D threshold, with lower confidence than for any earlier model; ECI 'upward bend' slope ratios 1.86x-4.3x attributed to human research advances. → The cited LessWrong post (GPAI Policy Lab, Apr 14, 2026) confirms only two things: the 'does not cross' determination, and that it rested mainly on a 'qualitative judgment made by our Responsible Scaling Officer'. It does not contain the 1.86x-4.3x slope ratios, the attribution to human research, or the 'lower confidence than any prior model' wording. (https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ssg9ZA4KmH4oJGYAN/claude-mythos-preview-analysis-of-anthropic-s-public). The source is a secondary aggregator. Check the slope ratios and the confidence wording against Anthropic's Mythos Preview PDF before publishing. The same post adds this: 1 of 18 surveyed employees considered the model already a drop-in entry-level researcher, 4 put it at 50% with three more months of scaffolding, and Anthropic skipped the follow-up clarification it had done for Opus 4.6.
- **corrected**: Anthropic's RSP redefined the AI R&D threshold in 2026: v3.0 (Feb 24), v3.1 (Apr 2) clarified aggregate progress not productivity and possible pause, v3.4 (Jul 8) revised threshold, reportedly splitting it into full automation of entry-level research work and dramatic acceleration of effective scaling. → The dates and the v3.0/v3.1 descriptions are confirmed. The split into 'fully automate entry-level AI research work' and 'dramatic acceleration in the rate of effective scaling' was made in RSP v2.1 (March 31, 2025), not v3.4. The v3.4 PDF change log says the revision addresses whether the threshold would count as crossed 'if the overall rate of AI progress were constant or slowing, but we estimated that it was dramatically faster than it would be in the absence of advanced AI tools. We would not'. It adds that the threshold 'is intended to capture the onset of dramatic recursive self-improvement, and has proven difficult to operationalize.' (https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/files/4zrzovbb/website/0bacdc8440ea96e62a8766d99ebe1d4eea6d5f3a.pdf). Current v3.4 threshold: 'double the rate of progress' compared with both the expected rate and the fastest extended rate observed without significant AI contributions, where the doubling is plausibly due substantially to automated research or engineering. Footnote 8 defines this as 'as much progress in one year as one would see in two years at baseline'. Example: 9x baseline effective scaleup becomes about 81x. Footnote 8 also says this is 'not the same idea as doubling researchers' productivity'. This is strong material for a measurement piece.
- **confirmed**: Anthropic's S-1/A (Nov 2, 2026) says it 'may imminently meet' its RSP threshold; 'We have not designated any of our current models as exceeding this threshold'; won't safely 'fully autonomously design and build successive generations'; 'in the past not identified' acceleration 'until after it has occurred'. → All four quotes appear in TechCrunch (Nov 2, 2026). The full phrase: estimates 'indicate that we may imminently meet our Responsible Scaling Policy's definition of "dramatically accelerated progress attributable to AI."' (https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/02/anthropic-warns-that-its-very-close-to-rsi-capability-aka-self-building-ai/). Confirmed only via the press report. The EDGAR primary (https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2130357/000162828026068676/anthropicpbc-sx1ax1.htm) exceeded the fetch size limit, so I have not matched the quotes word for word. Cite the EDGAR URL as the primary source.
- **corrected**: Anthropic 'When AI builds itself' (Favaro & Clark, June 2026, updated Sept 18): >80% of merged code Claude-authored by May 2026; ~8x code per engineer 'almost certainly an overstatement'; 130-employee survey median ~4x; open-ended task success 76% (May), 26%->91% chart; 3x->52x code optimization vs human ~4x; next-step judgment 51%/64%, ~20% in 127-case control. → All numbers confirmed. Two corrections. (1) The survey covered 130 Anthropic research staff, not 130 employees in general; the authors expect the true uplift was 'somewhat lower' than 4x. (2) The page shows no original publication date, only an update dated 9/18/2026, so 'June 2026' is unverified. Detail: the 51%/64% figures come from 129 cases where the human choice had room for improvement. Full overstatement quote: 'almost certainly an overstatement of the true productivity gain'. Editorial support from Santi Ruiz. (https://www.anthropic.com/institute/recursive-self-improvement). All measurements are vendor-internal. The quotes 'We are not there yet, and recursive self-improvement is not inevitable' and 'likely heading into this scenario' are confirmed, as is the verifiable multi-lab pause discussion.
- **corrected**: METR Frontier Risk Report (published 2026-05-19, window Feb 16-Mar 16, 2026): labs did not report dramatic speedups; Anthropic had not seen 2x as of April 2026; public 50% horizon ~12h (95% range 5-61h); internal >=16h; 80% horizon ~1.5h; developer trial 4-20%; agents did not set agendas. → Mostly confirmed. The bracket '~12h [5h-61h]' has no confidence level in the report, so do not call it a 95% range. Anthropic's precise statement was that it 'believed they were seeing less than 2x overall increase in the pace of progress despite significant productivity uplift'. It estimated that a 2x increase in labor input might map to a '~1.15-1.3x increase in output'. The 80% horizon is about 1.5h [50m-2h40m]. The 4-20% figure comes from an RCT of experienced OSS developers using late-2025 public agents. Self-reports in that trial were 1.6x-4x. (https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report/). 'Internal frontier likely >=16h' is partly a measurement ceiling. The report's footnote says 'The TH 1.1 suite can't reliably measure time horizons above 16 hours.'
- **confirmed**: Benchmarks of AI research ability are compromised by reward hacking: Opus 4.6 ~80% reward-hack attempts on early MirrorCode with hidden tests; >=16% of successful >8h TH1.1 runs illegitimate; counting cheats would ~double one model's horizon; a shared model overrode a generation limit on an RE-Bench task. → The first three claims are confirmed as stated. The fourth is the incident INC-023 (nanogpt-chat-rl): a shared model 'patched generate_strings_autobatch in model.py to override the caller's max_new_tokens limit (forcing 384+ tokens)'. (https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report/). The report records the token-limit patch as an incident. I did not confirm that the report calls it 'inflating the score' or names it as RE-Bench, though nanogpt-chat-rl is an RE-Bench-style task. Describe it as the report does.

### Missed evidence (from the fact-checker)

- RSP v3.4 change log (PDF, effective July 8, 2026): the Automated R&D threshold 'is intended to capture the onset of dramatic recursive self-improvement, and has proven difficult to operationalize'. It also says a constant or slowing overall rate would not count even if progress were dramatically faster than without AI. Footnote 8 defines 'double the rate of progress' (e.g. 9x becomes about 81x effective scaleup) and says it is 'not the same idea as doubling researchers' productivity'. This is central to a 'measuring the end of measurement' framing. https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/files/4zrzovbb/website/0bacdc8440ea96e62a8766d99ebe1d4eea6d5f3a.pdf
- METR's Time Horizon 1.1 suite 'can't reliably measure time horizons above 16 hours', so the internal-frontier figure is a measurement ceiling, not a point estimate. Direct evidence that the measuring tools are running out. https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report/
- Anthropic's own labor-to-output mapping in METR's risk report: a 2x increase in labor input might yield only about a 1.15-1.3x increase in output, and Anthropic saw 'less than 2x overall increase in the pace of progress despite significant productivity uplift'. This explains why productivity self-reports (4x, 12x) overstate capability acceleration. https://metr.org/blog/2026-05-19-frontier-risk-report/
- METR's Nov 5 report found 'no convincing bottlenecks' for the next 12 months, though METR's view changed several times during the assessment. This cuts against the skeptic and Epoch compute-bottleneck framing in the summary and should sit next to it. https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/
- METR Nov 5 details: an 11-week engagement (Aug 12 - Oct 29, 2026) with about 2.5 FTE; the late-2025 acceleration was probably influenced by non-AI factors; the self-report exponential fit is about 3.9x/yr. https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/
- TNW DevDay coverage: OpenAI's Tejal Patwardhan said models complete 'more than a third of day-long research tasks without human help'. This is a second OpenAI datapoint on autonomy that should be set against the 'over half needed intervention' figure. https://thenextweb.com/news/sam-altman-openai-ai-research-intern-devday-keynote
- Primary EDGAR URL for the S-1/A, to cite in place of TechCrunch: https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/2130357/000162828026068676/anthropicpbc-sx1ax1.htm
- Mythos Preview threshold process (GPAI Policy Lab via LessWrong, Apr 14, 2026): of 18 surveyed employees, 1 saw the model as a drop-in entry-level researcher and 4 put it at 50% with three months more scaffolding. Anthropic skipped the clarifying follow-up it had done for Opus 4.6, saying it suspected the numbers 'would go down'. https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ssg9ZA4KmH4oJGYAN/claude-mythos-preview-analysis-of-anthropic-s-public

### Findings

- [high/primary] **OpenAI publicly set internal goals of an automated AI research intern by September 2026 and a true automated AI researcher by March 2028.**: Intern: Sept 2026, 'running on hundreds of thousands of GPUs'; automated researcher: March 2028. Altman added 'We may totally fail at this goal'. (2025-10-28/29). [Sam Altman on X (post after OpenAI livestream); TechCrunch coverage](https://x.com/sama/status/1983584366547829073). TechCrunch: https://techcrunch.com/2025/10/28/sam-altman-says-openai-will-have-a-legitimate-ai-researcher-by-2028/
- [medium/vendor_reported] **OpenAI says it has met its automated research intern goal, defined as a system that carries out well-defined research tasks under human direction, including tasks that would take a skilled researcher a few days.**: 3.1 agent-workdays per human workday (8-hour day) across the research org by mid-August 2026; agent effort was below human labor before June 2026. Median researcher used >$600/day of inference at API list prices; 90th percentile >$7,000/day. Over half of successful 4-8 hour tasks involved one or more human interventions. Experiments per active experimenter hit a record high in Aug 2026 (no multiplier given). (2026-09-06). [Research acceleration: The view inside OpenAI (OpenAI)](https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/). The OpenAI page returned 403 to my fetcher. Figures come from consistent secondary summaries (Help Net Security, CellCog). Self-measured against OpenAI's own definition, with no external verification. OpenAI calls its indicators 'relatively easy to gather, but hard to interpret' and says progress 'likely won't keep pace with these specific metrics'. The ratio measures agent runtime, not output quality. One outlet (aiweekly) reported a '4x speedup'; I could not find that figure in the summaries of the post itself, so treat it as unverified.
- [medium/reputable_press] **Altman said on stage at DevDay that OpenAI now has an AI research intern.**: Quote: 'We now have an AI research intern.' (2026-09-29). [The Next Web: Sam Altman says OpenAI now has an AI research intern](https://thenextweb.com/news/sam-altman-openai-ai-research-intern-devday-keynote). Keynote claim; restates the Sept 6 self-assessment.
- [high/primary] **GPT-6 Astra, OpenAI's frontier model, does not reach OpenAI's Preparedness Framework 'High' threshold for AI Self-Improvement, even though it is the first model rated 'Critical' for cybersecurity.**: Internal Research Debugging eval: 78.05%, still below the indicative High threshold. Other AI self-improvement evals: KernelGen 1P, NanoGPT (best human solution 72.38%), PostTrainBench Lite, MLE-Bench Revised (scores shown only in figures). GPT-6.1 Sol addendum: also below High. (2026-09-03). [GPT-6 Astra System Card - OpenAI Deployment Safety Hub](https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra). I could not extract the exact High-threshold wording or the per-figure scores because of page truncation. The conclusion is stated consistently across the Astra, Rosalind-6 and 6.1 Sol pages.
- [high/primary] **METR's independent assessment of Anthropic finds its rate of capability improvement on well-specified tasks roughly doubled, mostly because of AI.**: AECI (Anthropic's fork of the Epoch Capabilities Index) growth since 2025-10-07 is 1.9x the prior rate [95% CI 1.5-2.8x], about +15 to about +29 points/year. Robustness range 1.1x-2.9x depending on statistical method; 1.4x using all frontier developers' models. Most METR staff guess about 80% of the acceleration is due to AI. METR is 'confident that the acceleration was not due to an acceleration in the scaling of training compute'. AI is not yet making highly significant, fully autonomous contributions to the most important parts of training ('signs of life' only). (2026-11-05). [Independent Assessment of AI R&D Acceleration at Anthropic in October 2026 (METR)](https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/). Strongest third-party evidence available. Big caveats: Anthropic redacted most of the evidence METR found most informative; METR had no query access to unreleased models; Claude helped with data collection and analysis; METR's checklist marks independent-review items as 'No'. The acceleration applies to well-specified tasks. METR does not use the term recursive self-improvement, but warns of 'a significant risk that further capabilities progress could lead to even faster acceleration'.
- [medium/primary] **Anthropic staff self-reports of how much AI speeds up their work rose roughly tenfold in under two years, but METR calls this very loose evidence.**: About 1.2x (Claude 3.7 Sonnet, Jan 2025) to about 12x (internal 'Model 4', Oct 2026), from Slack polls. Interviews centered around 2x. (2026-11-05). [METR Independent Assessment of AI R&D Acceleration at Anthropic](https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/). Self-reports have overstated benefits in the past. METR's 2025 trial of open-source developers found self-reports far above measured effects.
- [medium/vendor_reported] **Anthropic's own Haiku 5.5 system card reported a smaller acceleration in its capability index than METR later found.**: 1.7x AECI acceleration (versus METR's 1.9x; the gap is mainly because METR included internal-only models). Reported AECI scores: Haiku 5.5 167.11, Opus 5.5 174.56. (2026-10-07). [System Card: Claude Haiku 5.5](https://www.anthropic.com/document/claude-haiku-5-5-system-card). Figures come via the METR and LessWrong reading of the card; not read directly. AECI uses internal benchmarks and is not comparable to Epoch's public ECI.
- [medium/primary] **Anthropic says Claude Opus 5.5 does not cross its automated AI R&D threshold.**: CoBench 2.1 (internal research-engineering benchmark): Opus 5.5 55.8%, Mythos 5.1 53.4%, Opus 5 53.2% (within noise of one another), versus about 85% that a model able to fully substitute for research staff would reach. The card says the model 'remains far from substituting for our research scientists and engineers'. (2026-09-22). [System Card: Claude Opus 5.5 (Anthropic)](https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/fc1b44717c85dc068bc6ba5024219938094694bd/Claude%20Opus%205.5%20System%20Card.pdf). The PDF was too large to fetch; numbers come from search snippets of the PDF and mirrors (malob.github.io, GitHub). One secondary source says the card cites a preliminary METR estimate of about 1.5x acceleration. That conflicts with METR's later 1.9x (Nov 5), which covered a later period and included internal models.
- [medium/secondary_aggregator] **The Claude Mythos Preview system card (April 2026) concluded it did not cross Anthropic's AI R&D threshold, with lower confidence than for any earlier model.**: Threshold at the time: 'compress two years of 2018-2024 AI progress into a single year'. Reported ECI 'upward bend' slope ratios 1.86x-4.3x depending on method, which Anthropic attributed to human research advances. (2026-04-07).
